  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "huli-jing-c01",
      "source_id": "huli-jing-s1",
      "quote": "Huli jing (Chinese: 狐狸精) or Fox spirits, are Chinese mythological creatures usually capable of shapeshifting, who may either be benevolent or malevolent spirits.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Huli jing adalah roh rubah dalam mitologi Tiongkok yang dapat berubah wujud.",
        "en": "Huli jing are shapeshifting fox spirits of Chinese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "huli-jing-c02",
      "source_id": "huli-jing-s1",
      "quote": "Huli jing (Chinese: 狐狸精) or Fox spirits, are Chinese mythological creatures usually capable of shapeshifting, who may either be benevolent or malevolent spirits.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Roh rubah dapat bersifat baik atau jahat.",
        "en": "Fox spirits may be benevolent or malevolent."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "huli-jing-c03",
      "source_id": "huli-jing-s1",
      "quote": "Fox spirits and nine-tailed foxes appear frequently in Chinese folklore, literature, and mythology. Depending on the story, the fox spirit's presence may be a good or a bad omen. The motif of nine-tailed foxes from Chinese culture was eventually transmitted and introduced to Japanese, Korean, and Vietnamese cultures.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kehadirannya dapat menjadi pertanda baik maupun buruk, bergantung pada kisah.",
        "en": "Their presence may be a favorable or adverse omen depending on the tale."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "huli-jing-c04",
      "source_id": "huli-jing-s1",
      "quote": "The nine-tailed fox appears in the Shanhaijing (Classic of Mountains and Seas), compiled from the Warring States period to the Western Han period (circa fourth to circa first century BC). The work states:",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Shanhaijing memuat penyebutan rubah berekor sembilan.",
        "en": "The Shanhaijing contains references to nine-tailed foxes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "huli-jing-c05",
      "source_id": "huli-jing-s1",
      "quote": "In chapter 14 of the Shanhaijing, Guo Pu, a scholar of the Eastern Jin dynasty, had commented that the \"nine-tailed fox was an auspicious omen that appeared during times of peace.\" However, in chapter 1, another aspect of the nine-tailed fox is described:",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Guo Pu menafsirkan rubah berekor sembilan sebagai pertanda kedamaian.",
        "en": "Guo Pu interprets the nine-tailed fox as an auspicious sign of peace."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "huli-jing-c06",
      "source_id": "huli-jing-s1",
      "quote": "Three hundred li farther east is Qingqiu Mountain, where much jade can be found on its south slope and green cinnabar on its north. There is a beast here whose form resembles a fox with nine tails. It makes a sound like a baby and is a man-eater. Whoever eats it will be protected against insect-poison (gu).",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Versi dari Gunung Qingqiu menggambarkannya bersuara bayi dan memakan manusia.",
        "en": "The Qingqiu Mountain account describes an infant-like cry and man-eating behavior."
      }
    }
  ],

      "id": "Dokkaebi adalah makhluk legenda Korea yang berinteraksi dengan manusia melalui pertolongan atau tipu daya. Kisah Lady Dohwa dan Bachelor Bihyeong menjadi dokumentasi awal dokkaebi.",
      "en": "Dokkaebi are Korean legendary beings who interact with people through assistance or tricks. The tale of Lady Dohwa and Bachelor Bihyeong provides early documentation of dokkaebi.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "dokkaebi-c01",
        "dokkaebi-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Dokkaebi dibedakan dari hantu manusia; sebagian muncul melalui benda mati yang dirasuki. Beberapa kisah menggambarkan tantangan gulat ssireum kepada pengelana. Cahaya atau nyala biru dapat menandai kedatangan dokkaebi. Benda ajaibnya mencakup topi pemberi ketakterlihatan dan tongkat pemuncul barang.",

    {
      "id": "dokkaebi-c01",
      "source_id": "dokkaebi-s1",
      "quote": "Dokkaebi (Korean: 도깨비) are legendary creatures from Korean mythology and folklore. Dokkaebi, also known as \"Korean ogres\", are nature deities or spirits possessing extraordinary powers and abilities that are used to interact with humans, at times playing tricks on them and at times helping them.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dokkaebi adalah makhluk legenda Korea yang berinteraksi dengan manusia melalui pertolongan atau tipu daya.",
        "en": "Dokkaebi are Korean legendary beings who interact with people through assistance or tricks."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "dokkaebi-c02",
      "source_id": "dokkaebi-s1",
      "quote": "The earliest known documentation of dokkaebi is in the Silla-era tale of \"Lady Dohwa and Bachelor Bihyeong\" from the Memorabilia of the Three Kingdoms compiled during the Goryeo period. Dokkaebi are featured in many folk tale anthologies compiled during the Joseon period.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah Lady Dohwa dan Bachelor Bihyeong menjadi dokumentasi awal dokkaebi.",
        "en": "The tale of Lady Dohwa and Bachelor Bihyeong provides early documentation of dokkaebi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "dokkaebi-c03",
      "source_id": "dokkaebi-s1",
      "quote": "Dokkaebi are different from deities, divinities, spirits or ghosts, called gwishin (귀신) in Korean, in that they are not formed by the death of a human being, but rather by the spiritual possession of an inanimate object such as old discarded household tools like brooms, or objects stained with human blood.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dokkaebi dibedakan dari hantu manusia; sebagian muncul melalui benda mati yang dirasuki.",
        "en": "Dokkaebi differ from human ghosts; some arise through possession of inanimate objects."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "dokkaebi-c04",
      "source_id": "dokkaebi-s1",
      "quote": "Different versions of the dokkaebi mythology assign different attributes to them. In some cases, they are considered harmless but nevertheless mischievous, usually playing pranks on people or challenging wayward travelers to a ssireum (Korean wrestling) match for the right to pass. Dokkaebi are extremely skilled at wrestling and cannot be beaten unless their right side is exploited.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Beberapa kisah menggambarkan tantangan gulat ssireum kepada pengelana.",
        "en": "Some tales describe ssireum wrestling challenges to travelers."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "dokkaebi-c05",
      "source_id": "dokkaebi-s1",
      "quote": "Dokkaebi fire is a glimmering light or tall blue flames that herald the appearance of dokkaebi.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cahaya atau nyala biru dapat menandai kedatangan dokkaebi.",
        "en": "Glimmering light or blue flames may herald a dokkaebi's appearance."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "dokkaebi-c06",
      "source_id": "dokkaebi-s1",
      "quote": "Dokkaebi possess magical items, such as the dokkaebi hat called the dokkaebi gamtu (도깨비 감투), which grants the wearer the ability of invisibility, and the dokkaebi magic club called the dokkaebi bangmangi (도깨비 방망이), which can summon things and act functionally as a magic wand. Dokkaebi like buckwheat jelly, sorghum and red bean rice cakes, mashed sorghum, and the drinks.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 7",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Benda ajaibnya mencakup topi pemberi ketakterlihatan dan tongkat pemuncul barang.",
        "en": "Their magical objects include an invisibility hat and a club that summons things."
      }
    }
  ],

      "en": "Taotie is a Chinese mythological creature counted among the Four Perils. Bronze-vessel motifs depict a symmetrical animal mask with raised eyes.",

        "taotie-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Nama yang dipakai masyarakat Shang dan Zhou untuk motif tersebut tidak diketahui. Zuo Zhuan mengaitkan taotie dengan kerakusan.       "en": "The name used by Shang and Zhou people for the motif is unknown. The Zuo Zhuan associates taotie with gluttony. A quoted Lüshi Chunqiu passage describes a head without a body on Zhou bronzes. Li Zehou interprets taotie imagery as mysterious communication between people and heaven.",

      "title": "Taotie",
      "author": null,
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",
      "published": null,
      "language": "en",
      "type": "wikipedia",
      "accessed": "2026-10-01"
    }
  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "taotie-c01",
      "source_id": "taotie-s1",
      "quote": "The taotie is an ancient Chinese mythological creature that was commonly emblazoned on bronze and other artifacts during the 1st millennium BCE. Taotie are one of the Four Perils in Chinese classics like the Classic of Mountains and Seas, alongside the Hundun, Qiongqi, and Taowu.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Taotie adalah makhluk mitologis Tiongkok yang termasuk Empat Bahaya.",
        "en": "Taotie is a Chinese mythological creature counted among the Four Perils."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "taotie-c02",
      "source_id": "taotie-s1",
      "quote": "The Taotie is often represented as a motif on dings, which are Chinese ritual bronze vessels from the Shang (c. 1600 – c. 1050 BCE) and Zhou dynasties (c. 1046 – 256 BCE). The design typically consists of a zoomorphic mask, described as being frontal, bilaterally symmetrical, with a pair of raised eyes and typically no lower jaw area.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Motif pada bejana perunggu menampilkan topeng hewan simetris dengan mata menonjol.",
        "en": "Bronze-vessel motifs depict a symmetrical animal mask with raised eyes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "taotie-c03",
      "source_id": "taotie-s1",
      "quote": "Although modern scholars use the word taotie, it is actually not known what word the Shang and Zhou dynasties used to call the design on their bronze vessels; as American paleographer Sarah Allan notes, there is no particular reason to assume that the term taotie was known during the Shang period.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama yang dipakai masyarakat Shang dan Zhou untuk motif tersebut tidak diketahui.",
        "en": "The name used by Shang and Zhou people for the motif is unknown."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "taotie-c04",
      "source_id": "taotie-s1",
      "quote": "Although modern scholars use the word taotie, it is actually not known what word the Shang and Zhou dynasties used to call the design on their bronze vessels; as American paleographer Sarah Allan notes, there is no particular reason to assume that the term taotie was known during the Shang period.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Zuo Zhuan mengaitkan taotie dengan kerakusan.",
        "en": "The Zuo Zhuan associates taotie with gluttony."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "taotie-c05",
      "source_id": "taotie-s1",
      "quote": "The taotie on Zhou bronzes [ding] has a head but no body. When it eats people, it does not swallow them, but harms them.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kutipan Lüshi Chunqiu menggambarkan kepala tanpa tubuh pada perunggu Zhou.",
        "en": "A quoted Lüshi Chunqiu passage describes a head without a body on Zhou bronzes."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "taotie-c06",
      "source_id": "taotie-s1",
      "quote": "Li Zehou, a Chinese scholar of philosophy and intellectual history, thinks the description of the taotie in the Lüshi Chunqiu has a much deeper meaning, and that \"the meaning of taotie is not [about] 'eating people' but making a mysterious communication between people and Heaven (gods).\"",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 8",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Li Zehou menafsirkan motif taotie sebagai komunikasi misterius antara manusia dan langit.",
        "en": "Li Zehou interprets taotie imagery as mysterious communication between people and heaven."
      }
    }
  ],
  "conflicts": [],

    {

      "id": "Gagak berkaki tiga adalah makhluk mitologis Asia Timur yang mewakili matahari. Dalam kisah Tiongkok, nama Yangwu dan Jinwu merujuk pada gagak matahari.",
      "en": "The three-legged crow is an East Asian mythological creature representing the sun. Chinese tales use Yangwu and Jinwu for the sun crow.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "three-legged-crow-c01",
        "three-legged-crow-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Nama sanzuwu digunakan dalam mitologi Tiongkok dan disebut dalam Shanhaijing. Gagak dalam cakram matahari muncul pada lambang pakaian resmi kekaisaran. Gagak matahari sering diberi warna merah meski disebut gagak emas. Gagak ini juga digambarkan bersama Ibu Suri dari Barat.",
      "en": "Chinese mythology calls it sanzuwu, which is mentioned in the Shanhaijing. The crow in a solar disc appears among symbols on formal imperial garments. The sun crow is often colored red despite the name golden crow. This crow is also portrayed with the Queen Mother of the West.",

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "three-legged-crow-c01",
      "source_id": "three-legged-crow-s1",
      "quote": "The three-legged (or tripedal) crow is a mythological creature in various mythologies and arts of East Asia. It is believed to inhabit and represent the Sun.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gagak berkaki tiga adalah makhluk mitologis Asia Timur yang mewakili matahari.",
        "en": "The three-legged crow is an East Asian mythological creature representing the sun."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "three-legged-crow-c02",
      "source_id": "three-legged-crow-s1",
      "quote": "The Chinese have several versions of crow and crow-Sun tales. But the most popular depiction and myth of the Sun crow is that of the Yangwu or Jinwu, the \"golden crow\". It has also been found figured on ancient coins from Lycia and Pamphylia.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam kisah Tiongkok, nama Yangwu dan Jinwu merujuk pada gagak matahari.",
        "en": "Chinese tales use Yangwu and Jinwu for the sun crow."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "three-legged-crow-c03",
      "source_id": "three-legged-crow-s1",
      "quote": "In Chinese mythology and culture, the three-legged crow is called the sanzuwu (simplified Chinese: 三足乌; traditional Chinese: 三足烏; pinyin: sān zú wū; Cantonese Yale: saam1zuk1wu1; Shanghainese: sae tsoh u) and is present in many myths. It is also mentioned in the Shanhaijing. The earliest known depiction of a three-legged crow appears in Neolithic pottery of the Yangshao culture.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama sanzuwu digunakan dalam mitologi Tiongkok dan disebut dalam Shanhaijing.",
        "en": "Chinese mythology calls it sanzuwu, which is mentioned in the Shanhaijing."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "three-legged-crow-c04",
      "source_id": "three-legged-crow-s1",
      "quote": "The sanzuwu in a disc represents the sun and is also one of the Twelve Ornaments that is used in the decoration of formal imperial garments in ancient China.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gagak dalam cakram matahari muncul pada lambang pakaian resmi kekaisaran.",
        "en": "The crow in a solar disc appears among symbols on formal imperial garments."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "three-legged-crow-c05",
      "source_id": "three-legged-crow-s1",
      "quote": "The most popular depiction and myth of a sanzuwu is of a sun crow called the Yangwu (陽烏), more commonly referred to as the Jinwu (金烏; 'golden crow') or \"golden crow\". Even though it is described as a corvid, it is usually coloured red instead of black.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gagak matahari sering diberi warna merah meski disebut gagak emas.",
        "en": "The sun crow is often colored red despite the name golden crow."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "three-legged-crow-c06",
      "source_id": "three-legged-crow-s1",
      "quote": "The sanzuwu is also depicted with the Queen Mother of the West who are believed to be her messengers.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 8",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gagak ini juga digambarkan bersama Ibu Suri dari Barat.",
        "en": "This crow is also portrayed with the Queen Mother of the West."
      }
    }
  ],

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "yeren-c01",
      "source_id": "yeren-s1",
      "quote": "野人（やじん、中国語: 野人、英語: Yeren）とは、未確認動物（UMA）の一種。雪男やビッグフットと同様に、人間に近い体格で二足歩行をし、なおかつ体表が毛に覆われているという外見的特徴を持つとされる。このため、現存する猿人（ラマピテクスなど）あるいは原人ではないかという憶測がなされている。",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yeren adalah makhluk tak terkonfirmasi yang digambarkan mirip manusia, berbulu, dan berjalan dengan dua kaki.",
        "en": "Yeren is an unconfirmed creature described as hairy, human-like, and bipedal."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yeren-c02",
      "source_id": "yeren-s1",
      "quote": "目撃報告は中国、特に湖北省神農架地区に多く見られている。ただし、捕獲や射殺をしたという伝聞は残っているものの、実際に生物学的な検証が行われた例は知られていない。中国科学院が神農架地区で過去に学術調査を行ったが、野人を発見することはできなかった。中国・湖北省で、野人を追う研究団体HWMRAが、世界中から新たなメンバーを募っている（2010年10月10日現在）。",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Laporan penampakan terutama dikaitkan dengan Shennongjia di Hubei, Tiongkok.",
        "en": "Reported sightings are especially associated with Shennongjia in Hubei, China."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yeren-c03",
      "source_id": "yeren-s1",
      "quote": "目撃報告は中国、特に湖北省神農架地区に多く見られている。ただし、捕獲や射殺をしたという伝聞は残っているものの、実際に生物学的な検証が行われた例は知られていない。中国科学院が神農架地区で過去に学術調査を行ったが、野人を発見することはできなかった。中国・湖北省で、野人を追う研究団体HWMRAが、世界中から新たなメンバーを募っている（2010年10月10日現在）。",

      ]

  "claims": [

      "id": "bai-ze-c01",
      "source_id": "bai-ze-s1",
      "quote": "Bai Ze (simplified Chinese: 白泽; traditional Chinese: 白澤; pinyin: Báizé) is a mythical creature from ancient Chinese legends. During the Tang Dynasty, it was introduced to Japan with its name unchanged.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bai Ze adalah makhluk gaib legenda Tiongkok yang mengetahui persoalan roh dan dewa.",
        "en": "Bai Ze is a Chinese legendary being knowledgeable about spirits and deities."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bai-ze-c02",
      "source_id": "bai-ze-s1",
      "quote": "Bai Ze (simplified Chinese: 白泽; traditional Chinese: 白澤; pinyin: Báizé) is a mythical creature from ancient Chinese legends. During the Tang Dynasty, it was introduced to Japan with its name unchanged.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah menghubungkannya dengan gambar roh yang dibuat atas permintaan Kaisar Kuning.",
        "en": "A tale links it to pictures of spirits commissioned by the Yellow Emperor."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bai-ze-c03",
      "source_id": "bai-ze-s1",
      "quote": "In the Ming Dynasty book SanCai TuHui (三才圖會), Bai Ze’s appearance is described as having green hair on a loong head, with a horn growing on the top and the ability to fly. In the History of Yuan (元史), it is recorded as having the head of a tiger, red mane, loong’s body, and a horn. Bai Ze’s image in China combines features of both the loong and tiger.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "SanCai TuHui menggambarkan kepala naga dengan rambut hijau, tanduk, dan kemampuan terbang.",
        "en": "The SanCai TuHui describes a dragon head with green hair, a horn, and flight."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bai-ze-c04",
      "source_id": "bai-ze-s1",
      "quote": "In the Ming Dynasty book SanCai TuHui (三才圖會), Bai Ze’s appearance is described as having green hair on a loong head, with a horn growing on the top and the ability to fly. In the History of Yuan (元史), it is recorded as having the head of a tiger, red mane, loong’s body, and a horn. Bai Ze’s image in China combines features of both the loong and tiger.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "History of Yuan memberikan varian kepala harimau, surai merah, dan tubuh naga.",
        "en": "The History of Yuan gives a variant with a tiger head, red mane, and dragon body."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bai-ze-c05",
      "source_id": "bai-ze-s1",
      "quote": "The only existing evidence related to Bái Zé Tú in China is an incomplete Dunhuang manuscript. It is said to have been copied in the 9th or 10th century and titled “Baize-jing guai—tu” ‘Bai Ze Diagrams of Spectral Prodigies’ (白澤精惟圖), now kept at the National Library of France (P2682). This Dunhuang manuscript does not contain any drawings of Bai Ze.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Naskah Dunhuang yang tersisa membahas diagram roh dan tidak memuat gambar Bai Ze sendiri.",
        "en": "The surviving Dunhuang manuscript concerns spirit diagrams and contains no drawing of Bai Ze itself."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bai-ze-c06",
      "source_id": "bai-ze-s1",
      "quote": "In Japan, Bai Ze is also called Hakutaku. The oldest known depiction of Hakutaku appears in the Tiandi ruixiang zhi ‘Treatise on the Auspicious Signs of Heaven and Earth’ (天地瑞祥志), a work originated in China. This work is listed in late 9th-century Japanese bibliographies, while it is unknown in Chinese ones.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di Jepang, makhluk ini dikenal sebagai Hakutaku.",
        "en": "In Japan, this being is known as Hakutaku."
      }
    }
  ],

    {
      "id": "Manimekhala adalah dewi penjaga laut dalam mitologi Hindu-Buddhis Asia Tenggara. Dalam Mahajanaka Jataka, ia menyelamatkan Pangeran Mahajanaka dari kapal karam.",
      "en": "Manimekhala is a sea-guardian goddess in Southeast Asian Hindu-Buddhist mythology. In the Mahajanaka Jataka, she rescues Prince Mahajanaka from a shipwreck.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "manimekhala-c01",
        "manimekhala-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Nama Pali maṇīmekhalā berarti sabuk permata. Reliefnya ditemukan di Zothoke, Myanmar, dan berasal dari milenium pertama Masehi. Di Thailand dan Kamboja, ia dikaitkan dengan kilat dan laut. Kisah pengejaran Ramasura menjelaskan kilat melalui bola kristalnya dan guntur melalui kapak pengejar.",
      "en": "The Pali name maṇīmekhalā denotes a jeweled girdle. Reliefs of her from the first millennium CE were found at Zothoke, Myanmar. In Thailand and Cambodia, she is associated with lightning and the seas. The Ramasura pursuit tale explains lightning through her crystal ball and thunder through her pursuer's axe.",

  "cultural_context": null,

    {
      "id": "manimekhala-s1",

      "title": "Manimekhala",

  "claims": [

      "id": "manimekhala-c01",
      "source_id": "manimekhala-s1",
      "quote": "Manimekhala (Pali: Maṇīmekhalā) is a goddess in the Hindu-Buddhist mythology. She is regarded as a guardian of the seas, namely the Indian Ocean and the South China Sea as part of the mythology of Southeast Asia. She was placed by Cātummahārājika to protect virtuous beings from shipwreck.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Manimekhala adalah dewi penjaga laut dalam mitologi Hindu-Buddhis Asia Tenggara.",
        "en": "Manimekhala is a sea-guardian goddess in Southeast Asian Hindu-Buddhist mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "manimekhala-c02",
      "source_id": "manimekhala-s1",
      "quote": "She appears in several Buddhist stories including the Mahanipata Jataka (Mahajanaka Jataka), in which she rescues Prince Mahajanaka from a shipwreck.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam Mahajanaka Jataka, ia menyelamatkan Pangeran Mahajanaka dari kapal karam.",
        "en": "In the Mahajanaka Jataka, she rescues Prince Mahajanaka from a shipwreck."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "manimekhala-c03",
      "source_id": "manimekhala-s1",
      "quote": "In Pali, maṇīmekhalā refers to a girdle or belt of jewels. In Southeast Asia, she is known by various indigenized appellations, including as Mani Maykhala (မဏိမေခလာ) in Burmese, as Moni Mekhala (មណីមេខលា) or Neang Mekhala (នាងមេខលា) in Khmer; as Mani Mekkhala (มณีเมขลา) in Thai.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama Pali maṇīmekhalā berarti sabuk permata.",
        "en": "The Pali name maṇīmekhalā denotes a jeweled girdle."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "manimekhala-c04",
      "source_id": "manimekhala-s1",
      "quote": "Archaeological evidence of Manimekhala in the form of reliefs has been found in Zothoke, Myanmar (near Bilin), dating to the first millennium AD.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Reliefnya ditemukan di Zothoke, Myanmar, dan berasal dari milenium pertama Masehi.",
        "en": "Reliefs of her from the first millennium CE were found at Zothoke, Myanmar."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "manimekhala-c05",
      "source_id": "manimekhala-s1",
      "quote": "Manimekhala is seen in wat paintings across Mainland Southeast Asia depicting scenes from the Mahajanaka. In Thailand and Cambodia, she is considered a goddess of lightning and the seas.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Di Thailand dan Kamboja, ia dikaitkan dengan kilat dan laut.",
        "en": "In Thailand and Cambodia, she is associated with lightning and the seas."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "manimekhala-c06",
      "source_id": "manimekhala-s1",
      "quote": "The story of Manimekhala and Ramasura is mentioned many times in the classical literature of Cambodia and Thailand. It depicts Manimekhala along with Ramasura (usually considered a depiction of Parashurama) and Arjuna.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah pengejaran Ramasura menjelaskan kilat melalui bola kristalnya dan guntur melalui kapak pengejar.",
        "en": "The Ramasura pursuit tale explains lightning through her crystal ball and thunder through her pursuer's axe."
      }
    }
  ],

      "id": "Xiezhi adalah makhluk mitologis asal Tiongkok, bertanduk tunggal dan menyerupai sapi atau kambing. Makhluk ini dianggap memahami ujaran manusia dan membedakan benar dari salah.",
      "en": "Xiezhi is a Chinese-origin mythical creature resembling an ox or goat with one horn. This creature is credited with understanding human speech and distinguishing right from wrong.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "xiezhi-c01",
        "xiezhi-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Dalam kisah Lunheng, Gao Yao memerintahkannya menyeruduk terdakwa yang bersalah. Lunheng juga menyebut lukisan makhluk dan pejabat itu pada kantor pemerintahan. Nama xiezhi digunakan untuk topi pejabat hukum. Pada zaman Tang, topi xiezhi termasuk pakaian sensor untuk persidangan pemakzulan.",
      "en": "In the Lunheng tale, Gao Yao orders it to ram guilty defendants. The Lunheng also mentions their images painted in public offices. The name xiezhi was applied to judicial officials' hats. In the Tang period, xiezhi hats formed part of censors' attire for impeachment proceedings.",

      "quote": "The xiezhi (獬豸; xièzhì < Eastern Han Chinese *gɛʔ-ḍɛʔ) is a mythical creature of Chinese origin found throughout Sinospheric legends. It resembles an ox or goat, with thick dark fur covering its body, bright eyes and a single long horn on its forehead. It has great intellect and understands human speech.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Xiezhi adalah makhluk mitologis asal Tiongkok, bertanduk tunggal dan menyerupai sapi atau kambing.",

    {
      "id": "xiezhi-c02",
      "source_id": "xiezhi-s1",
      "quote": "The xiezhi (獬豸; xièzhì < Eastern Han Chinese *gɛʔ-ḍɛʔ) is a mythical creature of Chinese origin found throughout Sinospheric legends. It resembles an ox or goat, with thick dark fur covering its body, bright eyes and a single long horn on its forehead. It has great intellect and understands human speech.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Makhluk ini dianggap memahami ujaran manusia dan membedakan benar dari salah.",
        "en": "This creature is credited with understanding human speech and distinguishing right from wrong."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "xiezhi-c03",
      "source_id": "xiezhi-s1",
      "quote": "According to legend, the xiezhi, was a single-horned sheep or goat which had power to divine the guilt or innocence of a person. Gao Yao, the minister of justice for the legendary Emperor Shun employed the beast during criminal proceedings and he would command the sheep to ram (head-butt) the accused. The beast would ram the guilty, but spare the innocent.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam kisah Lunheng, Gao Yao memerintahkannya menyeruduk terdakwa yang bersalah.",
        "en": "In the Lunheng tale, Gao Yao orders it to ram guilty defendants."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "xiezhi-c04",
      "source_id": "xiezhi-s1",
      "quote": "In the same work (Lunheng), the legend is prefaced the remark that public offices are painted with the images of the beast and the minister.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Lunheng juga menyebut lukisan makhluk dan pejabat itu pada kantor pemerintahan.",
        "en": "The Lunheng also mentions their images painted in public offices."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "xiezhi-c05",
      "source_id": "xiezhi-s1",
      "quote": "As a symbol of traditional Chinese law, xiezhi has been promoted by the Chinese dynasties. The judicial hat (法冠) was also referred to as the xiezhi after the mythical sheep/ox. The xiezhi hat was part of the attire of censors (yushi) into the 8th century during the Tang period, especially for an impeachment trial.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama xiezhi digunakan untuk topi pejabat hukum.",
        "en": "The name xiezhi was applied to judicial officials' hats."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "xiezhi-c06",
      "source_id": "xiezhi-s1",
      "quote": "As a symbol of traditional Chinese law, xiezhi has been promoted by the Chinese dynasties. The judicial hat (法冠) was also referred to as the xiezhi after the mythical sheep/ox. The xiezhi hat was part of the attire of censors (yushi) into the 8th century during the Tang period, especially for an impeachment trial.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada zaman Tang, topi xiezhi termasuk pakaian sensor untuk persidangan pemakzulan.",

      "id": "Bixi adalah tokoh mitologi Tiongkok berupa naga bercangkang kura-kura. Ia disebut salah satu dari sembilan putra Raja Naga.",
      "en": "Bixi is a Chinese mythological figure portrayed as a dragon with a turtle shell. He is described as one of the Dragon King's nine sons.",

      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Patung Bixi menjadi landasan prasasti peringatan dan makam. Tradisi prasasti di atas kura-kura sudah ada paling lambat pada awal abad ketiga. Guci pemakaman Museum Nanjing bertanggal 272 menampilkan miniatur kura-kura pembawa prasasti. Pada zaman Ming, aturan Hongwu membatasi penggunaan prasasti berbasis kura-kura menurut pangkat.",
      "en": "Bixi sculptures serve as supports for commemorative and funerary stelae. The tortoise-mounted stele tradition existed by the early third century. A Nanjing Museum funerary jar dated 272 depicts a miniature stele-bearing tortoise. Ming-era Hongwu regulations restricted tortoise-based stelae according to rank.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "bixi-c03",

      "quote": "Bixi, or Bi Xi (Wade–Giles: Pi-hsi), is a figure from Chinese mythology. One of the nine sons of the Dragon King, he is depicted as a dragon with the shell of a turtle.",

      "source_id": "bixi-s1",
      "quote": "Bixi, or Bi Xi (Wade–Giles: Pi-hsi), is a figure from Chinese mythology. One of the nine sons of the Dragon King, he is depicted as a dragon with the shell of a turtle.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Patung Bixi menjadi landasan prasasti peringatan dan makam.",
        "en": "Bixi sculptures serve as supports for commemorative and funerary stelae."

    {
      "id": "bixi-c04",
      "source_id": "bixi-s1",
      "quote": "The tradition of tortoise-mounted stelae originated no later than early 3rd century (late Han dynasty). According to the 1957 survey by Chêng Tê-k'un (鄭徳坤),  the earliest extant tortoise-borne stele is thought to be the one at the tomb of Fan Min (樊敏), in Lushan County, Ya'an, Sichuan. Victor Segalen had earlier identified the stele as a Han dynasty monument.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tradisi prasasti di atas kura-kura sudah ada paling lambat pada awal abad ketiga.",

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bixi-c05",
      "source_id": "bixi-s1",
      "quote": "In the collection of the Nanjing Museum there is a hunping funerary jar, dating to 272 AD, with a miniature architectural composition on top, depicting, among other objects, a tortoise carrying a stele erected by the Jin dynasty governor of Changsha in honor of a local dignitary.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Guci pemakaman Museum Nanjing bertanggal 272 menampilkan miniatur kura-kura pembawa prasasti.",

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "bixi-c06",
      "source_id": "bixi-s1",
      "quote": "The bixi tradition flourished during the Ming and Qing dynasties. The Ming founder, the Hongwu Emperor, in the first year after the dynasty had been proclaimed (1368), adopted regulations, allowing tortoise-based funerary tablets to the higher ranks of the nobility and the mandarinate.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Pada zaman Ming, aturan Hongwu membatasi penggunaan prasasti berbasis kura-kura menurut pangkat.",

  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Snow Lion adalah hewan surgawi Tibet yang melambangkan pegunungan bersalju. Penampilannya lazim putih dengan surai pirus.",
      "en": "The Snow Lion is a Tibetan celestial animal symbolizing snowy mountains. It is commonly portrayed as white with a turquoise mane.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "snow-lion-c01",
        "snow-lion-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Singa salju digunakan pada lambang, uang, perangko, dan bendera Tibet. Cerita dan peribahasa menempatkannya sebagai raja hewan di gunung tertinggi. Kisah Tibet menyebut Gesar dan Milarepa dibesarkan oleh singa salju betina. Sebuah legenda mengatakan singa betina menghasilkan susu melalui telapak kakinya.",
      "en": "Snow lions appeared on Tibetan emblems, currency, stamps, and flags. Tales and proverbs place it as king of beasts in the highest mountains. Tibetan lore describes Gesar and Milarepa as raised by snow lionesses. One legend says the lioness produces milk through her paws.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "snow-lion-c03",
        "snow-lion-c04",
        "snow-lion-c05",
        "snow-lion-c06"

  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "snow-lion-c01",
      "source_id": "snow-lion-s1",
      "quote": "The Snow Lion (sometimes spelled snowlion; Tibetan: གངས་སེང་གེ་, Wylie: gangs seng ge; Chinese: 雪獅) is a celestial animal of Tibet. It is the emblem of Tibet, representing the snowy mountain ranges and glaciers of Tibet, and may also symbolize power and strength, and fearlessness and joy, east and the earth element. It is one of the Four Dignities.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Snow Lion adalah hewan surgawi Tibet yang melambangkan pegunungan bersalju.",
        "en": "The Snow Lion is a Tibetan celestial animal symbolizing snowy mountains."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "snow-lion-c02",
      "source_id": "snow-lion-s1",
      "quote": "It ranges over the mountains, and is commonly pictured as being white with a turquoise mane.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Penampilannya lazim putih dengan surai pirus.",
        "en": "It is commonly portrayed as white with a turquoise mane."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "snow-lion-c03",
      "source_id": "snow-lion-s1",
      "quote": "From 1909 until 1959, a single snow lion or a pair of them was used as the national emblem of Tibet on coins, postage stamps, banknotes and the national flag of Tibet. The version shown on right with two Snow Lions was introduced by the 13th Dalai Lama in 1912 based on old military banners, and is still used by the Government of Tibet in Exile.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Singa salju digunakan pada lambang, uang, perangko, dan bendera Tibet.",
        "en": "Snow lions appeared on Tibetan emblems, currency, stamps, and flags."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "snow-lion-c04",
      "source_id": "snow-lion-s1",
      "quote": "Snow lion is frequently mentioned in Tibetan folk songs and proverbs. It is thought to live in the highest mountains as it is the \"king of beasts\" that would tower over other animals of the lower levels. Snow lions may also represent hermits and yogis who live high up in the mountains.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cerita dan peribahasa menempatkannya sebagai raja hewan di gunung tertinggi.",
        "en": "Tales and proverbs place it as king of beasts in the highest mountains."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "snow-lion-c05",
      "source_id": "snow-lion-s1",
      "quote": "In Tibetan lore, two Tibetan culture heroes, Gesar and Milarepa, were said to have been raised by snow lioness. The milk of the Snow Lioness (Tibetan: Gangs Sengemo) is reputed to contain special nutrients to heal the body and restore it to harmony. Some holy medicinal remedies are believed to contain the essence of Snow Lioness milk.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah Tibet menyebut Gesar dan Milarepa dibesarkan oleh singa salju betina.",
        "en": "Tibetan lore describes Gesar and Milarepa as raised by snow lionesses."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "snow-lion-c06",
      "source_id": "snow-lion-s1",
      "quote": "Legend has it that the lioness produces milk from its paws, and the milk may pass into hollow balls given to the lioness to play with. This ball may be represented in Tibetan art as a three-coloured \"wheel of joy\" (dga' 'khyil).",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebuah legenda mengatakan singa betina menghasilkan susu melalui telapak kakinya.",
        "en": "One legend says the lioness produces milk through her paws."
      }
    }
  ],

      "en": "He Bo, or Hebo, is the Yellow River deity in Chinese mythology. Bingyi is another name for this river deity.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "he-bo-c01",
        "he-bo-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Hebo dapat dipahami sebagai personifikasi watak Sungai Kuning. Puisi Heavenly Questions memuat kisah Hou Yi menyerang Hebo dan mengambil istrinya Luoshen.       "en": "Hebo can be understood as a personification of the Yellow River's character. The Heavenly Questions poem contains a tale of Hou Yi attacking Hebo and taking his wife Luoshen.       "id": "he-bo-c01",
      "source_id": "he-bo-s1",
      "quote": "Hebo (Chinese: 河伯; lit. 'Lord of the River'), also known as Bingyi (冰夷), is the god of the Yellow River (Huang He). The Yellow River is the main river of northern China, one of the world's major rivers and a river of great cultural importance in China. This is reflected in Chinese mythology by the tales surrounding the deity Hebo.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "He Bo atau Hebo adalah dewa Sungai Kuning dalam mitologi Tiongkok.",
        "en": "He Bo, or Hebo, is the Yellow River deity in Chinese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "he-bo-c02",
      "source_id": "he-bo-s1",
      "quote": "Hebo (Chinese: 河伯; lit. 'Lord of the River'), also known as Bingyi (冰夷), is the god of the Yellow River (Huang He). The Yellow River is the main river of northern China, one of the world's major rivers and a river of great cultural importance in China. This is reflected in Chinese mythology by the tales surrounding the deity Hebo.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "religious-tradition",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bingyi termasuk nama lain dewa sungai ini.",
        "en": "Bingyi is another name for this river deity."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "he-bo-c03",
      "source_id": "he-bo-s1",
      "quote": "To some extent, the deity Hebo is a personification of the character of the Yellow River. However, Hebo has also had an important role in the history of religious worship in China (especially North China) and also having a more general function in terms of Chinese culture, including literature and poetry.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hebo dapat dipahami sebagai personifikasi watak Sungai Kuning.",
        "en": "Hebo can be understood as a personification of the Yellow River's character."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "he-bo-c04",
      "source_id": "he-bo-s1",
      "quote": "In the poem \"Heavenly Questions\" in the Chu Ci anthology, it is recorded: \"The Emperor sent Hou Yi to reform the people of Xia. Why did he shoot Hebo and take his wife Luoshen?\" The poem tells the story of Hòu Yì, a legendary archer who was sent by the Emperor to reform the people of Xia.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Puisi Heavenly Questions memuat kisah Hou Yi menyerang Hebo dan mengambil istrinya Luoshen.",
        "en": "The Heavenly Questions poem contains a tale of Hou Yi attacking Hebo and taking his wife Luoshen."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "he-bo-c05",
      "source_id": "he-bo-s1",
      "quote": "The character, Hé (河) may be used somewhat generically to refer to rivers in general or to various particular flowing bodies of water. In the case of Hebo, it is particularly and primarily associated with the Yellow River of China.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Unsur he pada namanya terutama mengacu pada Sungai Kuning.",
        "en": "The he element in his name particularly refers to the Yellow River."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "he-bo-c06",
      "source_id": "he-bo-s1",
      "quote": "The contemporary meaning of Bó (伯) is generally considered to be that of an honorific title, of a martial or noble designation, similar to the European titles of nobility rendered in English as \"count\" or \"earl\". It also means uncle, as in one’s father’s oldest brother.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bo dapat berfungsi sebagai gelar kehormatan yang berhubungan dengan pangkat bangsawan.",

    "id": "Nian adalah makhluk dalam kisah Tiongkok yang tinggal di laut atau gunung.",

    {
      "id": "Nian adalah makhluk dalam kisah Tiongkok yang tinggal di laut atau gunung. Catatan tertulis tentang Nian sebagai makhluk baru diketahui dari awal abad kedua puluh.",
      "en": "Nian is a beast in Chinese tales said to inhabit the sea or mountains. Written references to Nian as a creature are known only from the early twentieth century.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "nian-c01",
        "nian-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Kisah menyebutnya keluar pada tahun baru untuk memangsa manusia dan hewan. Penampilan Nian memiliki varian, termasuk singa berwajah datar dengan tubuh anjing. Legenda menjelaskan pemasangan lentera merah sebagai upaya menghindari serangan Nian. Praktik perayaan lebih dahulu tercatat dalam teks kuno tanpa menyebut makhluk bernama Nian.",
      "en": "The tale describes it emerging at New Year to feed on people and animals. Nian's appearance varies, including a flat-faced lion with a dog's body. A legend explains red lanterns as an attempt to avert Nian's attacks. Celebration practices were recorded in ancient texts without mentioning a creature named Nian.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "nian-c03",
        "nian-c04",
        "nian-c05",

      ]

      "quote": "A nian beast (traditional Chinese: 年獸; simplified Chinese: 年兽; Hanyu Pinyin: Nián shòu; Tongyong Pinyin: Nián shòu; Wade–Giles: Nien2 shou4; Cantonese Yale: Nìhn sau; Zhuyin Fuhao: ㄋㄧㄢˊ ㄕㄡˋ) is a beast in Chinese mythology.       "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nian adalah makhluk dalam kisah Tiongkok yang tinggal di laut atau gunung.",

    },
    {
      "id": "nian-c02",
      "source_id": "nian-s1",
      "quote": "A nian beast (traditional Chinese: 年獸; simplified Chinese: 年兽; Hanyu Pinyin: Nián shòu; Tongyong Pinyin: Nián shòu; Wade–Giles: Nien2 shou4; Cantonese Yale: Nìhn sau; Zhuyin Fuhao: ㄋㄧㄢˊ ㄕㄡˋ) is a beast in Chinese mythology. According to Chinese mythology, the nian lives under the sea or in the mountains. The Chinese character nian more usually means \"year\" or \"new year\".",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Catatan tertulis tentang Nian sebagai makhluk baru diketahui dari awal abad kedua puluh.",
        "en": "Written references to Nian as a creature are known only from the early twentieth century."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nian-c03",
      "source_id": "nian-s1",
      "quote": "Once every year at the beginning of Chinese New Year, the nian would come out of its hiding place to feed, mostly on people and animals. During the winter, when food was scarce, it would raid villages, eating the crops and sometimes the villagers themselves - particularly their children.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kisah menyebutnya keluar pada tahun baru untuk memangsa manusia dan hewan.",
        "en": "The tale describes it emerging at New Year to feed on people and animals."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nian-c04",
      "source_id": "nian-s1",
      "quote": "Once every year at the beginning of Chinese New Year, the nian would come out of its hiding place to feed, mostly on people and animals. During the winter, when food was scarce, it would raid villages, eating the crops and sometimes the villagers themselves - particularly their children.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Penampilan Nian memiliki varian, termasuk singa berwajah datar dengan tubuh anjing.",
        "en": "Nian's appearance varies, including a flat-faced lion with a dog's body."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nian-c05",
      "source_id": "nian-s1",
      "quote": "Some local legends attribute the Chinese lion dance (舞獅) to the nian. The tradition has its origins in a story of a nian's attack on a village. After the attack, the villagers discussed how to make the nian leave them in peace. Since it was discovered that the beast was afraid of the color red, people put red lanterns and spring scrolls on their windows and doors.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Legenda menjelaskan pemasangan lentera merah sebagai upaya menghindari serangan Nian.",
        "en": "A legend explains red lanterns as an attempt to avert Nian's attacks."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "nian-c06",
      "source_id": "nian-s1",
      "quote": "Various aspects of cultural practices relating to Chinese New Year are part of the nian legend. These cultural practices are recorded in ancient texts, though none of them refer to a creature called nian.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "scholarly-interpretation",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Praktik perayaan lebih dahulu tercatat dalam teks kuno tanpa menyebut makhluk bernama Nian.",
        "en": "Celebration practices were recorded in ancient texts without mentioning a creature named Nian."

      "en": "The Yellow Dragon is the animal-form manifestation of the Yellow Emperor in Chinese mythology. In Wuxing, this dragon embodies the earth element.",

        "yellow-dragon-c01",
        "yellow-dragon-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Sebuah legenda mengatakan ia muncul tanpa tanduk dari Sungai Luo di hadapan Fuxi. Dalam kisah itu, naga mengajarkan unsur tulisan kepada Fuxi.     {
      "id": "yellow-dragon-c01",
      "source_id": "yellow-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "The Yellow Dragon (simplified Chinese: 黄龙; traditional Chinese: 黃龍; pinyin: Huánglóng; Cantonese Yale: Wong4 Lung4)  is the zoomorphic incarnation of the Yellow Emperor of the center of the universe in Chinese religion and mythology.",

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yellow-dragon-c02",
      "source_id": "yellow-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "The Yellow Dragon is a part of Wuxing and the Four Symbols as the embodiment of the element of earth.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam Wuxing, naga ini melambangkan unsur bumi.",
        "en": "In Wuxing, this dragon embodies the earth element."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yellow-dragon-c03",
      "source_id": "yellow-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "According to legends, the Yellow Dragon already manifested hornless to Fuxi, emerging from the River Luo, and instructed him with the elements of writing. When he appeared before Fuxi, he filled a hole in the sky made by the monster Gonggong.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Sebuah legenda mengatakan ia muncul tanpa tanduk dari Sungai Luo di hadapan Fuxi.",
        "en": "One legend says it appeared hornless from the Luo River before Fuxi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yellow-dragon-c04",
      "source_id": "yellow-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "According to legends, the Yellow Dragon already manifested hornless to Fuxi, emerging from the River Luo, and instructed him with the elements of writing. When he appeared before Fuxi, he filled a hole in the sky made by the monster Gonggong.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam kisah itu, naga mengajarkan unsur tulisan kepada Fuxi.",
        "en": "In that tale, the dragon teaches elements of writing to Fuxi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yellow-dragon-c05",
      "source_id": "yellow-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "The Yellow Emperor was said to have turned into the form of the Yellow Dragon at the end of his life to ascend to Heaven. Since the Chinese consider him to be their ancestor, they sometimes refer to themselves as \"children of the dragon\" (also see: Nine sons of the dragon). This legend also contributed towards the use of the Chinese dragon as a symbol of imperial power.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Kaisar Kuning dipercaya berubah menjadi naga ini untuk naik ke langit pada akhir hidupnya.",
        "en": "The Yellow Emperor is said to become this dragon to ascend to heaven at life's end."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yellow-dragon-c06",
      "source_id": "yellow-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "The Chinese national flag from 1889 to 1912 is also called the Yellow Dragon Flag (黄龙旗; 黃龍旗; huánglóng qí).",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {

    "id": "Hundun adalah makhluk tanpa wajah sekaligus gambaran kekacauan purba dalam mitologi Tiongkok.",
    "en": "Hundun is a faceless legendary being and an image of primordial chaos in Chinese mythology.",
    "claim_ids": [
      "hundun-c01"
    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Hundun adalah makhluk tanpa wajah sekaligus gambaran kekacauan purba dalam mitologi Tiongkok. Istilah hundun juga berkembang menjadi kata untuk keadaan kacau atau tak terpahami.",
      "en": "Hundun is a faceless legendary being and an image of primordial chaos in Chinese mythology. The term hundun also developed senses of disorder or unintelligibility.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "hundun-c01",
        "hundun-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Tulisan namanya memiliki varian dalam Zhuangzi dan Zuozhuan. Dalam kisah Zhuangzi, Hundun menjadi penguasa wilayah tengah yang menjamu Shu dan Hu. Shu dan Hu melubangi tubuh Hundun untuk memberinya tujuh bukaan, tetapi ia mati pada hari ketujuh. Gambaran lain menyerupai kantong kuning dengan enam kaki, empat sayap, dan tanpa wajah.",
      "en": "Its written name has variants in the Zhuangzi and Zuozhuan. In a Zhuangzi tale, Hundun rules the central region and generously hosts Shu and Hu. Shu and Hu bore seven openings into Hundun, but he dies on the seventh day. Another account resembles a yellow sack with six feet, four wings, and no face.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "hundun-c03",
        "hundun-c04",
        "hundun-c05",
        "hundun-c06"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],
  "variants": [],
  "stories": [],

  "timeline": [],

  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "hundun-s1",
      "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hundun",

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "hundun-c01",
      "source_id": "hundun-s1",
      "quote": "Hundun (Chinese: 混沌; pinyin: Hùndùn; Wade–Giles: Hun4-tun4; lit. 'muddled confusion') is both a \"legendary faceless being\" in Chinese mythology and the \"primordial and central chaos\" in Chinese cosmogony, comparable with the world egg.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hundun adalah makhluk tanpa wajah sekaligus gambaran kekacauan purba dalam mitologi Tiongkok.",
        "en": "Hundun is a faceless legendary being and an image of primordial chaos in Chinese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hundun-c02",
      "source_id": "hundun-s1",
      "quote": "Hundun 混沌 was semantically extended from a mythic \"primordial chaos; nebulous state of the universe before heaven and earth separated\" to mean \"unintelligible; chaotic; messy; mentally dense; innocent as a child\".",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Istilah hundun juga berkembang menjadi kata untuk keadaan kacau atau tak terpahami.",
        "en": "The term hundun also developed senses of disorder or unintelligibility."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hundun-c03",
      "source_id": "hundun-s1",
      "quote": "While hùndùn \"primordial chaos\" is usually written as 混沌 in contemporary sources, it is also written as 渾沌—as in the Daoist classic Zhuangzi—or 渾敦 —as in the Zuozhuan.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Tulisan namanya memiliki varian dalam Zhuangzi dan Zuozhuan.",
        "en": "Its written name has variants in the Zhuangzi and Zuozhuan."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hundun-c04",
      "source_id": "hundun-s1",
      "quote": "The emperor of the South Sea was called Shu [Brief], the emperor of the North Sea was called Hu [Sudden], and the emperor of the central region was called Hun-tun [Chaos]. Shu and Hu from time to time came together for a meeting in the territory of Hun-tun, and Hun-tun treated them very generously. Shu and Hu discussed how they could repay his kindness.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 21",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dalam kisah Zhuangzi, Hundun menjadi penguasa wilayah tengah yang menjamu Shu dan Hu.",
        "en": "In a Zhuangzi tale, Hundun rules the central region and generously hosts Shu and Hu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hundun-c05",
      "source_id": "hundun-s1",
      "quote": "The emperor of the South Sea was called Shu [Brief], the emperor of the North Sea was called Hu [Sudden], and the emperor of the central region was called Hun-tun [Chaos]. Shu and Hu from time to time came together for a meeting in the territory of Hun-tun, and Hun-tun treated them very generously. Shu and Hu discussed how they could repay his kindness.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 21",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Shu dan Hu melubangi tubuh Hundun untuk memberinya tujuh bukaan, tetapi ia mati pada hari ketujuh.",
        "en": "Shu and Hu bore seven openings into Hundun, but he dies on the seventh day."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "hundun-c06",
      "source_id": "hundun-s1",
      "quote": "There is a god here who looks like a yellow sack. He is scarlet like cinnabar fire. He has six feet and four wings. He is Muddle Thick. He has no face and no eyes. He knows how to sing and dance. He is in truth the great god Long River.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 34",
      "context": "historical-record",

    }

'Toad') or \"Zhaocai Chan Chu\" (Chinese: 招财蟾蜍; pinyin: zhāocái chánchú; lit. 'wealth-beckoning toad'), is most commonly translated as \"Money Toad\" or \"Money Frog\".     {
      "id": "jin-chan-c02",
      "source_id": "jin-chan-s1",
      "quote": "This mythical creature is said to appear during the full moon, near houses or businesses that will soon receive good news (most of the time, the nature of this good news is understood to be wealth-related).",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "jin-chan-c03",
      "source_id": "jin-chan-s1",
      "quote": "The Jin Chan is usually depicted as a bullfrog with red eyes, flared nostrils and only one hind leg (for a total of three legs), sitting on a pile of traditional Chinese cash, with a coin in its mouth. On its back, it often displays seven diamond spots. According to feng shui beliefs, Jin Chan helps attract and protect wealth, and guards against bad luck.",

      "id": "jin-chan-c05",
      "source_id": "jin-chan-s1",
      "quote": "The Jin Chan is a legendary animal of the Han people. The money toad is associated with the Daoist monk, Liu Haichan, as the xianren's animal companion.",

        "kuafu-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Ia mengejar matahari dari timur ke barat dan meminum Sungai Kuning serta Sungai Wei. Air sungai tidak menghilangkan hausnya dan ia mati akibat kekurangan air. Tongkat kayunya berubah menjadi hutan pohon persik. Versi lain mengisahkan tubuhnya menjadi pegunungan.",

      ]

    {
      "id": "Hwanung memberi beruang dan harimau makanan serta larangan terkena matahari. Harimau meninggalkan gua, tetapi beruang bertahan lalu berubah menjadi perempuan. Ungnyeo berdoa di bawah pohon suci untuk memperoleh anak. Kisah pendirian ini tercatat dalam Samguk Yusa yang ditulis Ilyon.",

      "source_id": "ungnyeo-s1",
      "quote": "Ungnyeo (Korean: 웅녀; Hanja: 熊女; lit. 'bear woman') was a bear who became a woman according to the creation myth of the Korean nation. She has an important role in the myth as the wife of Hwanung, the divine king of the world, and as the mother of Dangun, the mythological founder of Korea.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ungnyeo adalah beruang yang menjadi perempuan dalam mitos pendirian Korea.",

      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ungnyeo-c02",
      "source_id": "ungnyeo-s1",
      "quote": "Ungnyeo (Korean: 웅녀; Hanja: 熊女; lit. 'bear woman') was a bear who became a woman according to the creation myth of the Korean nation. She has an important role in the myth as the wife of Hwanung, the divine king of the world, and as the mother of Dangun, the mythological founder of Korea.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ia menjadi istri Hwanung dan ibu Dangun.",
        "en": "She becomes Hwanung's wife and Dangun's mother."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ungnyeo-c03",
      "source_id": "ungnyeo-s1",
      "quote": "In the tale, a tiger and a bear lived together in a cave and prayed to the divine king Hwanung to be made human. Hwanung heard their prayers and gave them each 20 cloves of garlic and a bundle of mugwort, and ordered them to stay out of the sunlight and eat only this food for 100 days. Due to hunger, the tiger left the cave after roughly 20 days, but the bear remained inside.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Hwanung memberi beruang dan harimau makanan serta larangan terkena matahari.",
        "en": "Hwanung gives a bear and tiger food and instructions to avoid sunlight."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ungnyeo-c04",
      "source_id": "ungnyeo-s1",
      "quote": "Due to hunger, the tiger left the cave after roughly 20 days, but the bear remained inside. After 21 days, she was transformed into a woman.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Harimau meninggalkan gua, tetapi beruang bertahan lalu berubah menjadi perempuan.",
        "en": "The tiger leaves the cave, while the bear remains and becomes a woman."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ungnyeo-c05",
      "source_id": "ungnyeo-s1",
      "quote": "Ungnyeo was grateful and made offerings to Hwanung. Her lack of a husband drove her to depression, and she began to pray beneath a sacred betula tree (신단수; 神檀樹) to be blessed with a child. Hwanung heard her prayers and was deeply moved. He took Ungnyeo as his wife and soon after, she gave birth to a son, Dangun, who would go on to found the nation of Korea.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ungnyeo berdoa di bawah pohon suci untuk memperoleh anak.",
        "en": "Ungnyeo prays beneath a sacred tree for a child."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "ungnyeo-c06",
      "source_id": "ungnyeo-s1",
      "quote": "The earliest form of the myth is found at the beginning of the Samguk Yusa, a part-mythological, part-historical chronicle of Korea’s Three Kingdoms Period (primarily focusing on the kingdom of Silla).       "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "historical-record",

        "id": "Kisah pendirian ini tercatat dalam Samguk Yusa yang ditulis Ilyon.",

      }
    }
  ],

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "yinglong-c01",
      "source_id": "yinglong-s1",
      "quote": "Yinglong (traditional Chinese: 應龍; simplified Chinese: 应龙; pinyin: yìnglóng; lit. 'responsive dragon') is a winged dragon and rain deity in ancient Chinese mythology.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Yinglong adalah naga bersayap dan dewa hujan dalam mitologi Tiongkok kuno.",
        "en": "Yinglong is a winged dragon and rain deity of ancient Chinese mythology."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yinglong-c02",
      "source_id": "yinglong-s1",
      "quote": "This legendary creature's name combines yìng 應 \"respond; correspond; answer; reply; agree; comply; consent; promise; adapt; apply\" and lóng 龍 \"Chinese dragon\". Although the former character is also pronounced (with a different tone) yīng 應 \"should; ought to; need to; proper; suitable\", yinglong 應龍 definitively means \"responsive dragon; responding dragon\" and not \"proper dragon\".",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Nama Yinglong bermakna naga yang merespons.",
        "en": "The name Yinglong means a responding dragon."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yinglong-c03",
      "source_id": "yinglong-s1",
      "quote": "The Chinese classics frequently mention yinglong 應龍 \"a winged rain-dragon\" in myths about the Three Sovereigns and Five Emperors, especially the Yellow Emperor and his alleged descendant King Yu. The examples below, limited to books with English translations, are roughly arranged in chronological order, although some heterogeneous texts have uncertain dates of composition.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Teks klasik menghubungkannya dengan Kaisar Kuning dan Raja Yu.",
        "en": "Classical texts associate it with the Yellow Emperor and King Yu."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yinglong-c04",
      "source_id": "yinglong-s1",
      "quote": "The (3rd–2nd centuries BCE) Chu Ci \"Songs of Chu\" mentions Yinglong helping King Yu 禹, the legendary founder of the Xia dynasty, to control the mythic Great Deluge. According to Chinese mythology, Emperor Yao 堯 assigned Yu's father Gun 鯀, who was supposedly a descendant of the Yellow Emperor, to control massive flooding, but he failed.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Chu Ci menyebut Yinglong membantu Yu mengendalikan banjir besar.",
        "en": "The Chu Ci mentions Yinglong helping Yu control the great deluge."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yinglong-c05",
      "source_id": "yinglong-s1",
      "quote": "The Heavenly Questions section (3, 天問) asks about Yinglong, in context with Zhulong 燭龍 \"Torch Dragon\".",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Bagian Heavenly Questions membahas Yinglong dalam rangkaian pertanyaan kosmologis.",
        "en": "The Heavenly Questions section discusses Yinglong among cosmological questions."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "yinglong-c06",
      "source_id": "yinglong-s1",
      "quote": "When Lord Gun brought forth Yu from his belly, how was he transformed? Yu inherited the same tradition and carried on the work of his father.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "historical-record",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Puisi itu menanyakan apa yang digoreskan naga bersayap pada tanah saat penanganan banjir.",
        "en": "The poem asks what the winged dragon traced on the ground during flood control."
      }
    }
  ],

    "claim_ids": [

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Peng adalah burung raksasa dalam mitologi Tiongkok yang berubah dari ikan Kun. Aksara peng memakai unsur burung, sedangkan kun memakai unsur ikan.",
      "en": "Peng is a giant Chinese mythological bird transformed from the fish Kun. The peng character uses a bird component, while kun uses a fish component.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "peng-c01",
        "peng-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Peng dahulu juga dipakai sebagai varian aksara feng dalam fenghuang. Dapeng dan Dapengniao termasuk sebutan lain yang berarti Peng besar atau burung Peng besar.",
      "en": "Peng was formerly used as a character variant for feng in fenghuang. Dapeng and Dapengniao are alternative names denoting the great Peng or great Peng bird.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "peng-c03",

      ]

  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],
  "weaknesses": [],

  "sources": [
    {
      "id": "peng-s1",
      "url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peng_(mythology)",
      "title": "Peng (mythology)",
      "author": null,
      "publisher": "Wikipedia",

      "language": "en",

  ],
  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "peng-c01",
      "source_id": "peng-s1",
      "quote": "A Peng (Chinese: 鵬; pinyin: péng) or Dapeng (大鵬; dàpéng) is a giant bird that transforms from a Kun (鯤; kūn), a giant fish, in Chinese mythology.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Peng adalah burung raksasa dalam mitologi Tiongkok yang berubah dari ikan Kun.",
        "en": "Peng is a giant Chinese mythological bird transformed from the fish Kun."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "peng-c02",
      "source_id": "peng-s1",
      "quote": "The Chinese logograms for peng and kun exemplify common radical-phonetic characters. Peng (鵬) combines the \"bird radical\" (鳥) with a peng (朋; 'friend') phonetic, and kun (鲲; 鯤) combines the \"fish radical\" (魚) with a kun (昆; 'progeny', 'insect') phonetic.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Aksara peng memakai unsur burung, sedangkan kun memakai unsur ikan.",
        "en": "The peng character uses a bird component, while kun uses a fish component."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "peng-c03",
      "source_id": "peng-s1",
      "quote": "Both the mythic Chinese Peng and Kun names involve word play. Peng (鵬) was anciently a variant Chinese character for feng (鳳) in fenghuang (鳳凰; \"Chinese phoenix\"), as in the ca. 100 CE Shuowen Jiezi; Kun (鯤) originally meant \"fish roe; fry; spawn\" (ca. 200 BCE Erya).",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Peng dahulu juga dipakai sebagai varian aksara feng dalam fenghuang.",
        "en": "Peng was formerly used as a character variant for feng in fenghuang."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "peng-c04",
      "source_id": "peng-s1",
      "quote": "Synonyms of Peng include Dapeng (大鵬; \"Big Peng\", \"Great Peng\") and Dapengniao (大鵬鳥; \"Great Peng Bird\"). Dapeng is also a place name for a few places in greater China, most notably in Shenzhen and Taiwan.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "etymology",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Dapeng dan Dapengniao termasuk sebutan lain yang berarti Peng besar atau burung Peng besar.",
        "en": "Dapeng and Dapengniao are alternative names denoting the great Peng or great Peng bird."
      }
    }
  ],

      "en": "Korean dragons are legendary creatures of Korean mythology and folklore. Mir or Miri are Korean names for dragons.",

    {
      "id": "Naga ini terutama dikaitkan dengan air, pertanian, hujan, dan awan. Legenda Munmu mengisahkan keinginannya menjadi naga Laut Timur untuk melindungi kerajaan.       "en": "These dragons are primarily associated with water, agriculture, rain, and clouds. The Munmu legend recounts his wish to become an East Sea dragon protecting the kingdom.   "claims": [
    {
      "id": "korean-dragon-c01",
      "source_id": "korean-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "Korean dragons (Korean: 용/룡) are legendary creatures in Korean mythology and folklore. It is also called Mir (미르) or Miri (미리) in Korean.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Naga Korea adalah makhluk legenda dalam mitologi dan cerita rakyat Korea.",
        "en": "Korean dragons are legendary creatures of Korean mythology and folklore."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "korean-dragon-c02",
      "source_id": "korean-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "Korean dragons (Korean: 용/룡) are legendary creatures in Korean mythology and folklore. It is also called Mir (미르) or Miri (미리) in Korean.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mir atau Miri termasuk nama naga dalam bahasa Korea.",
        "en": "Mir or Miri are Korean names for dragons."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "korean-dragon-c03",
      "source_id": "korean-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "Whereas most dragons in European mythology are linked to the elements of fire and destruction, dragons in Korean mythology are primarily benevolent beings related to water and agriculture, often considered bringers of rain and clouds. Hence, many Korean dragons are said to have resided in rivers, lakes, oceans, or even deep mountain ponds.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Naga ini terutama dikaitkan dengan air, pertanian, hujan, dan awan.",
        "en": "These dragons are primarily associated with water, agriculture, rain, and clouds."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "korean-dragon-c04",
      "source_id": "korean-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "One particular Korean legend speaks of the great King Munmu, who on his deathbed wished to become a \"Great dragon of the East Sea in order to protect his kingdom\".",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 4",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Legenda Munmu mengisahkan keinginannya menjadi naga Laut Timur untuk melindungi kerajaan.",
        "en": "The Munmu legend recounts his wish to become an East Sea dragon protecting the kingdom."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "korean-dragon-c05",
      "source_id": "korean-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "The Korean dragon is in many ways very similar in appearance to other East Asian dragons such as the Chinese and Japanese dragons. It differs from the Chinese dragon in that it developed a longer beard.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 5",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Naga Korea digambarkan memiliki janggut lebih panjang dibanding naga Tiongkok.",
        "en": "Korean dragons are described with longer beards than Chinese dragons."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "korean-dragon-c06",
      "source_id": "korean-dragon-s1",
      "quote": "Occasionally a dragon may be depicted as carrying a giant orb known as the yeouiju (여의주), the Korean name for the mythical Cintamani, in its claws or its mouth.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Penggambaran dapat menyertakan bola yeouiju pada cakar atau mulutnya.",
        "en": "Depictions may include a yeouiju orb in the claws or mouth."
      }
    }
  ],

    },
    {
      "id": "Nüwa memotong empat kaki Ao untuk menopang langit yang rusak. Versi lain menempatkannya di Laut Bohai membawa tiga pulau makhluk abadi pada punggung. Ao dianggap memengaruhi tokoh Bixi yang berwujud campuran naga dan kura-kura.",

      "quote": "Ao (traditional Chinese: 鰲; simplified Chinese: 鳌; pinyin: áo < Old Chinese (ZS): *ŋaːw) is a large marine turtle in Chinese mythology.     "claim_ids": [

    ]
  },
  "long_description": [
    {
      "id": "Gwisin adalah roh orang mati dalam cerita rakyat Korea, berbeda dari dokkaebi. Ikatan dengan dunia hidup, seperti balas dendam atau menjaga orang tersayang, dapat menahan roh di bumi.",
      "en": "Gwisin are spirits of dead people in Korean folklore, distinct from dokkaebi. Ties to the living, such as revenge or care for loved ones, may keep a spirit on earth.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "gwisin-c01",
        "gwisin-c02"
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "Balas dendam menjadi tema umum cerita tentang gwisin. Cheonyeo-gwisin adalah roh perempuan yang meninggal tanpa menikah. Mul-gwisin adalah roh orang yang tenggelam.",
      "en": "Revenge is a common theme in gwisin tales. Cheonyeo-gwisin is the spirit of a woman who died unmarried. Mul-gwisin is the spirit of a drowned person.",
      "claim_ids": [
        "gwisin-c03",
        "gwisin-c04",
        "gwisin-c05"
      ]
    }
  ],
  "cultural_context": null,
  "etymology": null,
  "story_mode": null,
  "did_you_know": null,
  "abilities": [],

  "sources": [

  "claims": [
    {
      "id": "gwisin-c01",
      "source_id": "gwisin-s1",
      "quote": "In Korean folklore, Gwisin (Korean: 귀신) are a type of deity, divinity, spirit or ghost. They are considered similar to a yogoe (Korean: 요괴) or mamul (Korean: 마물). Unlike dokkaebi, gwisin are humans who have died.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 1",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Gwisin adalah roh orang mati dalam cerita rakyat Korea, berbeda dari dokkaebi.",
        "en": "Gwisin are spirits of dead people in Korean folklore, distinct from dokkaebi."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "gwisin-c02",
      "source_id": "gwisin-s1",
      "quote": "According to folklore, gwisin may be found in many places. It is claimed that when an individual dies but still has ties to the world of the living, such as in the case of revenge or caring for a loved one, their spirit remains on earth to complete the task before going on to the underworld.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 2",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Ikatan dengan dunia hidup, seperti balas dendam atau menjaga orang tersayang, dapat menahan roh di bumi.",
        "en": "Ties to the living, such as revenge or care for loved ones, may keep a spirit on earth."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "gwisin-c03",
      "source_id": "gwisin-s1",
      "quote": "There are a lot of legends about gwisin. Because they are a common form of ghost, children often make them up to scare others or parents tell stories to their children to teach them a lesson. The most common plot of a legend about gwisin is revenge, for example revenge for the family of the ghost. Another popular trope includes men who cheat on their wives, and murder someone.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 3",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Balas dendam menjadi tema umum cerita tentang gwisin.",
        "en": "Revenge is a common theme in gwisin tales."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "gwisin-c04",
      "source_id": "gwisin-s1",
      "quote": "The Cheonyeo-gwisin (처녀귀신) is the gwisin of a woman who died unmarried and usually have white Hanbok (한복) which are worn for funerals.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Cheonyeo-gwisin adalah roh perempuan yang meninggal tanpa menikah.",
        "en": "Cheonyeo-gwisin is the spirit of a woman who died unmarried."
      }
    },
    {
      "id": "gwisin-c05",
      "source_id": "gwisin-s1",
      "quote": "The Mul-gwisin (물귀신) is the spirit of someone who drowned.",
      "locator": "Article text, paragraph 6",
      "context": "traditional-belief",
      "statement": {
        "id": "Mul-gwisin adalah roh orang yang tenggelam.",
        "en": "Mul-gwisin is the spirit of a drowned person."
      }
    }
  ],
